# Excalidraw Data

## Text Elements
In machine learning, an algorithm is entirely useless without high-quality, structured training data. For computer vision, researchers use ImageNet. For natural language processing, they use datasets like Common Crawl. For computational drug discovery, you use ChEMBL. ^XLCZybtD

ChEMBL - is an enormous, manually curated open database maintained by the European Bioinformatics Institute. It contains over 2.9 million unique chemical compounds and over 24 million records of how those compounds interact with biological systems. ^43UHe6Dh

Target  - The specific biological protein the drug is tested against.This defines the scope of your project. 

You will usually filter ChEMBL to pull data for one specific target to train a specialized model.

Bioactivity (IC50)A measurement of potency.

 IC50 (Half-maximal inhibitory concentration) is the concentration of a drug required to inhibit a biological process by 50%.This is your target variable . 

A lower IC50 means higher potency (you need very little drug to jam the lock). Your model is ultimately trying to predict this number for unseen molecules. ^qgbfXuo3

If you design a drug that perfectly binds to the disease target (an amazing IC50 score), but it instantly kills the patient's liver cells, it's a useless drug. ^Gk7vrXrW

In computational drug discovery, predicting efficacy (does it work?) is just step one. We also have to predict a set of flags known as ADMET: ^PzyFmQmE

(A)bsorption: Can it get into the bloodstream? ^ObIq2z1t

(D)istribution: Can it reach the right organ (like crossing the blood-brain barrier)? ^HH73R79o

(M)etabolism: Will the liver destroy it too quickly? ^lCG8oh7l

(E)xcretion: Can the body get rid of it eventually? ^9vPqqzFm

(T)oxicity: Is it poisonous? ^EqgXCwmx
